**Bashar Hafez al-Assad**[^cite_note-4] (born 11 September 1965) is a former Syrian politician, medical doctor, military officer, and dictator[^cite_note-MF-5][^cite_note-dictator2-6] who served as the [president of Syria](516106.md) from 2000 until [his overthrow](78548066.md) in 2024 after the [Syrian civil war](30741795.md).[^cite_note-ARF-7][^cite_note-AFTM-8][^cite_note-AMGA-9] He is the son of [Hafez al-Assad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hafez_al-Assad), who ruled Syria from 1970 to 2000. His rule as president was a highly [personalist dictatorship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dictatorship#Personalist)[^cite_note-MF-5][^cite_note-dictator2-6] that governed Syria as a [totalitarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Totalitarian) [police state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Police_state),[^cite_note-10] becoming arguably more [repressive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_repression) than his father.[^cite_note-HRW-11] In August 2026, Assad was convicted of war crimes and crimes against humanity by a Syrian court and sentenced to death *[in absentia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/In_absentia_\(law\))*.[^cite_note-12][^cite_note-13]

In the 1980s, Assad became a doctor, and in the early 1990s he was training in [London](17867.md) as an [ophthalmologist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ophthalmologist). In 1994, after his elder brother [Bassel al-Assad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bassel_al-Assad) died in a car crash, Assad was recalled to Syria to take over Bassel's role as [heir apparent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heir_apparent). Assad entered the military academy and in 1998 took charge of the [Syrian occupation of Lebanon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_occupation_of_Lebanon) begun by [his father](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hafez_al-Assad). On 17 July 2000, Assad became president, succeeding his father, who had died on 10 June 2000.[^cite_note-14] Assad also became [commander-in-chief](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commander-in-chief) of the [Syrian Arab Armed Forces](78567599.md) and secretary-general of the [Central Command](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regional_Command_of_the_Arab_Socialist_Ba'ath_Party_%E2%80%93_Syria_Region) of the [Arab Socialist Ba'ath Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ba'ath_Party_\(Syrian-dominated_faction\)). Hopes that the Western-educated Assad would bring reform to Syria and relax the occupation of Lebanon[^cite_note-15] were dashed following a series of crackdowns in 2001–2002 that ended the [Damascus Spring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Damascus_Spring), a period defined by calls for transparency and democracy.[^cite_note-HRW-11]

Assad's first decade in power was marked by [extensive censorship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Censorship_in_Syria), summary executions, [forced disappearances](686148.md), discrimination against ethnic minorities, and extensive surveillance by the [Ba'athist secret police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_Intelligence_Directorate_\(Syria\)). While the Assad government described itself as [secular](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secular_state), various political scientists and observers noted that his regime exploited [sectarian tensions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sectarianism) in the country. Although Assad inherited Hafez's power structures and [personality cult](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Personality_cult), he lacked the loyalty received by his father and faced rising discontent against his rule. As a result, many people from his father's regime resigned or were purged, and the political inner circle was replaced by staunch loyalists from [Alawite](436616.md) clans. Assad's early [economic liberalisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_liberalization) programs worsened inequalities and centralised the socio-political power of the loyalist Damascene elite of the [Assad family](27334130.md), alienating the Syrian rural population, urban working classes, businessmen, industrialists, and people from traditional Ba'ath strongholds. Assad was forced to end the Syrian occupation of Lebanon during the [Cedar Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cedar_Revolution) in 2005, which was triggered by [the assassination of Lebanese prime minister Rafic Hariri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assassination_of_Rafic_Hariri). The [Mehlis report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mehlis_report) implicated Assad's regime in the assassination, with a particular focus on [Maher al-Assad](3139640.md), [Assef Shawkat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assef_Shawkat), [Hassan Khalil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hassan_Khalil), [Bahjat Suleiman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bahjat_Suleiman), and [Jamil Al Sayyed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jamil_Al_Sayyed).[^cite_note-16]

After the [Syrian revolution](39750126.md) began in 2011, Assad led a deadly crackdown against [Arab Spring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Spring) protests which led to the outbreak of the [Syrian civil war](30741795.md). The [Syrian opposition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_opposition), [United States](3434750.md), [European Union](9317.md), and the majority of the [Arab League](52625.md) called on him to resign, but he refused and the war escalated. Between 2011 and 2024, over 600,000 people were killed, with pro-Assad forces causing more than 90% of civilian casualties.[^cite_note-17] Throughout the war, the [Ba'athist Syrian armed forces](78567599.md) carried out several [chemical attacks](39668667.md).[^cite_note-18] In 2013, the [United Nations](31769.md) (UN) [High Commissioner for Human Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_Commissioner_for_Human_Rights) stated that findings from [a UN inquiry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independent_International_Commission_of_Inquiry_on_the_Syrian_Arab_Republic) directly implicated Assad in [crimes against humanity](61099.md). The regime's [perpetration of war crimes](36355222.md#Ba'athist_Syrian_Armed_Forces_and_allied_forces) led to international condemnation and isolation,[^cite_note-19] although Assad maintained power with assistance from Syria's longtime allies [Iran](14653.md) and [Russia](25391.md). Iran [launched a military intervention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_intervention_in_the_Syrian_civil_war) in support of his government in 2013 and Russia [followed in 2015](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_intervention_in_the_Syrian_civil_war); by 2021, Assad's regime had regained control over most of the country. In November 2024, a coalition of Syrian rebels mounted [several offensives](78460726.md) with the intention of ousting Assad.[^cite_note-20][^cite_note-21] On the morning of 8 December 2024, as rebel troops first entered Damascus, Assad fled to [Moscow](19004.md) and was granted [political asylum](1621432.md) by the [Russian government](9787373.md).[^cite_note-\:2-22][^cite_note-23] Later that day, [Damascus fell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_Damascus_\(2024\)) to rebel forces, and [Assad's regime collapsed](78548066.md).[^cite_note-ARF-7][^cite_note-AFTM-8][^cite_note-AMGA-9]

Assad's regime committed systemic [human rights violations](78548501.md) and [war crimes](48626.md), making it [one of the most repressive regimes in modern times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_totalitarian_regimes). The regime was consistently ranked among the "worst of the worst" within [Freedom House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_House) indexes.[^cite_note-24] In 2025, France issued three arrest warrants against Assad in connection with the bombing of a civilian-populated area in Daraa in 2017 that killed Franco-Syrian dual national Salah Abou Nabout,[^cite_note-auto4-25][^cite_note-26] the 2012 killings of journalists [Marie Colvin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marie_Colvin) and [Remi Ochlik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Remi_Ochlik) in Homs,[^cite_note-auto3-27][^cite_note-28] and the [Ghouta chemical attacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghouta_chemical_attack) in 2013.[^cite_note-29][^cite_note-30] In September 2025, a Syrian court issued an arrest warrant for Assad over the killing of civilians during the [Siege of Daraa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Daraa) in 2011, paving the way for its circulation through [Interpol](14338129.md). The arrest warrant cited charges such as "premeditated murder", "[torture](47702.md) leading to death", and "deprivation of liberty".[^cite_note-31][^cite_note-32][^cite_note-33]

## Early life, family and education

Further information: [Al-Assad family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Assad_family)

Bashar Hafez al-Assad was born in Damascus on 11 September 1965, as the second son and third child of [Anisa Makhlouf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anisa_Makhlouf) and [Hafez al-Assad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hafez_al-Assad).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZisser200720-34][^cite_note-35] *al-Assad* in [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic) means "*the lion*". Assad's paternal grandfather, [Ali al-Assad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali_al-Assad), had managed to change his status from peasant to minor notable and, to reflect this, in 1927 he had changed the family name from *al-Wahsh* (lit. 'the Savage') to *al-Assad*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESealeMcConville19926-36]

Assad's father, Hafez al-Assad, was born to an impoverished rural family of [Alawite](436616.md) background and rose through the [Ba'ath Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ba'ath_Party_\(Syrian-dominated_faction\)) ranks to take control of the [Syrian branch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Socialist_Ba'ath_Party_%E2%80%93_Syria_Region) of the Party in the [Corrective Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corrective_Revolution_\(Syria\)), culminating in his rise to the [Syrian presidency](516106.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMikaberidze201338-37] Hafez promoted his supporters within the Ba'ath Party, many of whom were also of Alawite background.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZisser200720-34][^cite_note-guardian-38] After the revolution, Alawite strongmen were installed while [Sunnis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni), [Druze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Druze), and [Ismailis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ismaili) were removed from the army and Ba'ath party.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoosa1987305-39] Hafez al-Assad's 30-year military rule witnessed the transformation of Syria into a dynastic dictatorship. The new political system was led by the Ba'ath party elites dominated by the Alawites, who were fervently loyal to the Assad family and controlled the military, security forces and secret police.[^cite_note-Allam,_Saber_2019_26–27-40][^cite_note-41]

The younger Assad had five siblings, three of whom are deceased. A sister named Bushra died in infancy.[^cite_note-New_Republic_2013-42] Assad's younger brother, Majd, was not a public figure and little is known about him other than he was [intellectually disabled](18567040.md),[^cite_note-Bar-43] and died in 2009 after a "long illness".[^cite_note-44]

Unlike his brothers Bassel and [Maher](3139640.md), and second sister, also named [Bushra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bushra_al-Assad), Bashar was quiet, reserved and lacked interest in politics or the military.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZisser200721-45][^cite_note-Bar-43][^cite_note-Republic-46] The Assad children reportedly rarely saw their father,[^cite_note-Roula-47] and Bashar later stated that he only entered his father's office once while he was president.[^cite_note-48] He was described as "soft-spoken",[^cite_note-49] and according to a university friend, he was timid, avoided eye contact and spoke in a low voice.[^cite_note-50]

Assad received his primary and secondary education in the Arab-French al-Hurriya School in Damascus.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZisser200721-45] In 1982, he graduated from high school and then studied medicine at [Damascus University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Damascus_University).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELeverett200559-51]

## Medical career and rise to power



Photograph of [Bassel al-Assad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bassel_al-Assad) (1962–1994), Bashar's older brother, who was initially destined to succeed his father in the [presidency of Syria](516106.md), but died in an automobile accident in 1994

In 1988, Assad graduated from medical school and began working as an army doctor at Tishrin Military Hospital on the outskirts of Damascus.[^cite_note-Ladno-52][^cite_note-\:6-53] Four years later, he settled in London to start postgraduate training in [ophthalmology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ophthalmology) at [Western Eye Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Eye_Hospital).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELeverett200560-54] He was described as a "geeky I.T. guy" during his time in London.[^cite_note-55] Bashar had few political aspirations,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMinahan200283-56] and his father had been grooming Bashar's older brother Bassel as future president.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETuckerRoberts2008167-57] Shortly after Bassel died in a car accident in 1994, Bashar was recalled to the Syrian Army. State propaganda soon began elevating Bashar's public image as "the hope of the masses" to prepare the public for a continuation of the rule of the [Assad dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assad_dynasty).[^cite_note-58][^cite_note-59]

Soon after the death of Bassel, Hafez al-Assad decided to make Bashar the new [heir apparent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heir_apparent).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZisser200735-60] Over the next six and a half years, until his death in 2000, Hafez prepared Bashar for succession. General [Bahjat Suleiman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bahjat_Suleiman), an officer in the [Defense Companies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defense_Companies_\(Syria\)), was entrusted with overseeing preparations for a smooth transition,[^cite_note-61][^cite_note-Roula-47] which were made on three levels. First, support was built up for Bashar in the military and security apparatus. Second, Bashar's image was established with the public. And lastly, Bashar was familiarised with the mechanisms of running the country.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELeverett200561-62]

To establish his credentials in the military, Bashar entered the [military academy at Homs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homs_Military_Academy) in 1994 and was propelled through the ranks to become a colonel of the elite [Syrian Republican Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_Republican_Guard) in January 1999.[^cite_note-Ladno-52][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZisser200730-63][^cite_note-64] To establish a power base for Bashar in the military, old divisional commanders were pushed into retirement, and new, young, Alawite officers with loyalties to him took their place.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMa'ozGinatWinckler199941-65]

Young Bashar al-Assad serving in the military

Young Bashar al-Assad serving in the military

In 1998, Bashar took charge of Syria's [Lebanon file](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_occupation_of_Lebanon), which had since the 1970s been handled by Vice President [Abdul Halim Khaddam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdul_Halim_Khaddam), who had until then been a potential contender for president.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMa'ozGinatWinckler199941-65] By taking charge of Syrian affairs in Lebanon, Bashar was able to push Khaddam aside and establish his own power base in Lebanon.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZisser200734–35-66] In the same year, after minor consultation with Lebanese politicians, Bashar installed [Emile Lahoud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emile_Lahoud), a loyal ally of his, as the [President of Lebanon](2032981.md) and pushed former [Lebanese Prime Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lebanese_Prime_Minister) [Rafic Hariri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rafic_Hariri) aside, by not placing his political weight behind his nomination as prime minister.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlanford200669–70-67] To further weaken the old Syrian order in Lebanon, Bashar replaced the long-serving de facto Syrian [High Commissioner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_Commissioner) of Lebanon, [Ghazi Kanaan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghazi_Kanaan), with [Rustum Ghazaleh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rustum_Ghazaleh).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlanford200688-68]

Parallel to his military career, Bashar was engaged in public affairs. He was granted wide powers and became head of the bureau to receive complaints and appeals of citizens, and led a campaign against corruption. As a result of this campaign, many of Bashar's potential rivals for president were put on trial for corruption.[^cite_note-Ladno-52] Bashar also became the President of the [Syrian Computer Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_Computer_Society) and helped to introduce the internet in Syria, which aided his image as a moderniser and reformer. [Ba'athist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ba'athism) loyalists in the party, military and the [Alawite](436616.md) sect were supportive of Bashar al-Assad, enabling him to become his father's successor.[^cite_note-facing_down_rebellion-69]

## Presidency

Main article: [Presidency of Bashar al-Assad](69538970.md)

See also: [Ba'athist Syria](57134800.md)

### Early presidency (2000–2011)

After the death of Hafez al-Assad on 10 June 2000, the [Constitution of Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Syria) was amended. The minimum age requirement for the presidency was lowered from 40 to 34, which was Bashar's age at the time.[^cite_note-70] The sole candidate of the presidential referendum, Assad was subsequently [confirmed president on 10 July 2000](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000_Syrian_presidential_election), with 97.29% support for his leadership.[^cite_note-WaPo_%-71][^cite_note-Reuters_%-72][^cite_note-CBS_%-73] In line with his role as President of Syria, he was also appointed the [commander-in-chief](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commander-in-chief) of the [Syrian Armed Forces](27479.md) and Regional Secretary of the Ba'ath Party.[^cite_note-facing_down_rebellion-69] A series of [state elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elections_in_Syria) were held every seven years which Assad won with overwhelming majority of votes. The elections are unanimously regarded by independent observers as a [sham process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Election#Shams) and boycotted by the [opposition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_opposition_to_Bashar_al-Assad).[^cite_note-77][^cite_note-84] The last two elections – held in 2014 and 2021 – were conducted only in areas controlled by the Syrian government during the country's ongoing civil war and condemned by the [United Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UN).[^cite_note-theguardian.com-85][^cite_note-telegraph.co.uk-86][^cite_note-Kossaify-87] In the five years before 2011 the government of Bashar consolidated state power over the economy and political system. This consolidation went hand in hand with measures which were filled with nepotism and corruption.[^cite_note-\:7-88]

#### Damascus Spring

See also: [Damascus Spring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Damascus_Spring)

Immediately after he took office, a reform movement known as [Damascus Spring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Damascus_Spring) led by writers, intellectuals, dissidents, cultural activists, etc. made cautious advances, which led to the closing of [Mezzeh prison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mezzeh_prison) and the declaration of a wide-ranging amnesty releasing hundreds of [Muslim Brotherhood](20742.md) affiliated political prisoners.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELeverett200580-89] However, security crackdowns commenced again within the year, turning it into the Damascus Winter.[^cite_note-90][^cite_note-Ghadry-91] Hundreds of intellectuals were arrested, targeted, exiled or sent to prison and the state of emergency was continued. The early concessions were rolled back to tighten authoritarian control, censorship was increased and the Damascus Spring movement was banned under the pretext of "national unity and stability". The regime's policy of a "social market economy" became a symbol of corruption, as Assad loyalists became its sole beneficiaries.[^cite_note-facing_down_rebellion-69][^cite_note-France_24-92][^cite_note-93][^cite_note-94] Several discussion forums were shut down and many intellectuals were abducted by the Mukhabarat, tortured and killed. Many analysts believe that initial promises of opening up were part of a government strategy to find Syrians who were not supportive of the new leadership.[^cite_note-Ghadry-91]



Assad with Brazilian President [Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva](99328.md) in Damascus, 3 December 2003

During a state visit by [British Prime Minister](24150.md) [Tony Blair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tony_Blair) to Syria in October 2001, Bashar publicly condemned the [United States invasion of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_invasion_of_Afghanistan) in a joint press conference, stating that "\[w]e cannot accept what we see every day on our television screens – the killing of innocent civilians. There are hundreds dying every day." Assad also praised [Palestinian militant groups](2646043.md) as "[freedom fighters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_fighter)" and [criticised Israel](30074296.md) and [the Western world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Western_sentiment) during the conference. British officials subsequently described Assad's political views as being more conciliatory in private, claiming that he criticised the [September 11 attacks](5058690.md) and accepted the [legitimacy of the State of Israel](40229800.md).[^cite_note-95]

According to investigative journalist [Seymour Hersh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seymour_Hersh), "Syria had emerged as one of the [CIA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CIA)'s most effective intelligence allies in the fight against [al-Qaeda](1921.md)" following the [September 11 attacks](5058690.md) and during the early stages of the US-led [war on terror](13425800.md).[^cite_note-The_New_Yorker_Syrian_Bet-96] [^cite_note-aeifrum-97] Hersh also quoted [Flynt Leverett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flynt_Leverett), former Senior Director for Middle East Affairs, as saying that "the quality and quantity of information from Syria exceeded the agency's expectations, but that from the Syrians' perspective, they got little in return for it."[^cite_note-leverettinterview-98][^cite_note-The_New_Yorker_Syrian_Bet-96] Syria closely cooperated with the CIA's [detention and interrogation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CIA_black_sites) program of people deemed "[illegal enemy combatants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illegal_enemy_combatants)"; Syrian prisons were a major site of [extraordinary rendition](1031191.md) by the CIA of alleged al-Qaeda members where they were tortured by Syrian interrogators on behalf of the CIA.[^cite_note-The_New_Yorker_2005-99][^cite_note-Al_Bawaba_2013-100][^cite_note-Washington_Post_2013-101] According to a 2013 report by the [Open Society Foundations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Open_Society_Foundations), Syria was one of the "most common destinations for rendered suspects" under the CIA's program.[^cite_note-102]

#### Assassination of Rafic Hariri and Cedar Revolution

See also: [Assassination of Rafic Hariri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assassination_of_Rafic_Hariri), [Cedar Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cedar_Revolution), and [Syrian occupation of Lebanon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_occupation_of_Lebanon)

> "It will be [Lahoud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emile_Lahoud).. opposing him is tantamount to opposing [Assad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assad_dynasty) himself.. I will break [Lebanon](17771.md) over your head and over [Walid Jumblatt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walid_Jumblatt)'s head. So you had better return to [Beirut](37428.md) and arrange the matter on that basis."

— Assad's threats to [Rafic Hariri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rafic_Hariri) in August 2004, over the issue of tenure extension of Syrian ally Emile Lahoud

[^cite_note-103]



The crime-scene in [Beirut](37428.md) where Hariri and 21 others were killed in a terrorist attack in February 2005. The area was cordoned off to conduct an international investigation.

On 14 February 2005, [Rafic Hariri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rafic_Hariri), the former prime minister of Lebanon, was [assassinated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assassination_of_Rafic_Hariri) in a massive truck-bomb explosion in [Beirut](37428.md), killing 22 people. *[The Christian Science Monitor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Christian_Science_Monitor)* reported that "Syria was widely blamed for Hariri's murder. In the months leading to the assassination, relations between Hariri and Syrian President Bashar al-Assad plummeted amid an atmosphere of threats and intimidation."[^cite_note-104] Bashar promoted his brother-in-law [Assef Shawkat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assef_Shawkat), a key figure suspected of orchestrating the terrorist attack, as the chief of [Syrian Military Intelligence Directorate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_Intelligence_Directorate_\(Syria\)) immediately after Hariri's death.[^cite_note-105]



Protesters take to the streets during Lebanon's "Independence Intifada", also known as the [Cedar Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cedar_Revolution).

The killings caused massive uproar, triggering an *[intifada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intifada)* in [Lebanon](17771.md) and hundreds of thousands of protestors poured on the streets to demand total withdrawal of Syrian military forces. After mounting international pressure that called Syria to implement the [UNSC Resolution 1559](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Security_Council_Resolution_1559), Bashar al-Assad declared on 5 March that he would order the departure of Syrian soldiers. On 14 March 2005, more than a million [Lebanese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lebanese_people) protestors – Muslims, Christians, and Druze – demonstrated in Beirut, marking the monthly anniversary of Hariri's murder. UN Resolution 1595, adopted on 7 April, sent an [international commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assassination_of_Rafic_Hariri#Investigation) to investigate the assassination of Hariri. By 5 May 2005, United Nations had officially confirmed the total departure of all Syrian soldiers, ending the 29-year old [military occupation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_occupation_of_Lebanon). The uprisings that occurred in these months came to be known as Lebanon's "independence intifada" or the "[Cedar Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cedar_Revolution)".[^cite_note-106]

UN investigation commission's report published on 20 October 2005 revealed that high-ranking members of [Syrian intelligence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_intelligence) and Assad family had directly supervised the killing.[^cite_note-107][^cite_note-108][^cite_note-109] The [BBC](19344654.md) reported in December 2005 that "Damascus has strongly denied involvement in the car bomb which killed Hariri in February".[^cite_note-110]



Assad with Indian Prime Minister [Manmohan Singh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manmohan_Singh) in New Delhi, India, 18 June 2008

On 27 May 2007, Assad was [approved for another seven-year term](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007_Syrian_presidential_election) in a referendum on his presidency, with 97.6% of the votes supporting his continued leadership.[^cite_note-111][^cite_note-112][^cite_note-113] Opposition parties were not allowed in the country and Assad was the only candidate in the referendum.[^cite_note-CBS_%-73] Syria's opposition parties under the umbrella of [Damascus Declaration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Damascus_Declaration) denounced the elections as illegitimate and part of the regime's strategy to sustain the "[totalitarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Totalitarianism) system".[^cite_note-WaPo_%2-114][^cite_note-Reuters_%2-115] Elections in Ba'athist Syria were officially designated by the state as the event of "renewing the pledge of allegiance" to the Assads and voting was enforced by the Ba'athist military apparatus as a compulsory duty for every citizen. Announcement of the results were typically followed by pro-Assad rallies conducted by the Ba'ath party across the country extolling the regime, wherein participants were forced to declare their "devotion" to the President and celebrate "the virtues" of the Assad dynasty.[^cite_note-116][^cite_note-117][^cite_note-118]

Syria began developing a covert [nuclear weapons programme](13895017.md#Nuclear_program) with assistance of [North Korea](21255.md) during the 2000s, but its suspected [nuclear reactor](22151.md) was destroyed by the [Israeli Air Force](177619.md) during [Operation Outside the Box](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Orchard) in September 2007.[^cite_note-119][^cite_note-ifimes.org-120][^cite_note-121] Immediately after U.S. diplomatic efforts by Middle East envoy [George J. Mitchell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_J._Mitchell) in September 2010 to forge a regional peace, the Iranian president [Mahmoud Ahmadinejad](7907317.md) awarded Assad with his country's highest honor, the Islamic Republic Medal, with an aim to counter the effort.[^cite_note-122]

### Syrian civil war (2011–2024)

See also: [Arab Spring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Spring), [2011 Syrian Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_Syrian_Revolution), [Syrian civil war](30741795.md), and [Sanctions against Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanctions_against_Syria)



Anti-Assad demonstrations in [Douma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Douma,_Syria), 8 April 2011

[Protests in Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_uprising_phase_of_the_Syrian_Civil_War) began on 26 January 2011 following the [Arab Spring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Spring) protests that called for political reforms and the reinstatement of civil rights, as well as an end to the [state of emergency](298608.md) which had been in place since 1963.[^cite_note-123] One attempt at a "day of rage" was set for 4–5 February, though it ended uneventfully.[^cite_note-124] Protests on 18–19 March were the largest to take place in Syria for decades, and the Syrian authority responded with violence against its protesting citizens.[^cite_note-125] In his first public response to the protests delivered on 30 March 2011, Assad blamed the unrest on "conspiracies" and accused the Syrian opposition and protestors of seditious "*[fitna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fitna_\(word\))*", toeing the party-line of framing the Ba'athist state as the victim of an international plot. He also derided the [Arab Spring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Spring) movement, and described those participating in the protests as "germs", foreign agents and [fifth-columnists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fifth_column).[^cite_note-126][^cite_note-Farge-127][^cite_note-ohchr.org-128]

> "Throughout the speech, al-Assad remained faithful to the basic ideological line of [Syrian Baathism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-Ba'athism): the binary opposition of a devilishly determined, conspiring 'outside' bent on hurting a heroically defending and essentially good 'inside'... consistent with [Baathist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baathist) dualism, \[the speech] makes the sparing, if not grudging, mention of supposedly minor dissent in this 'inside'. This dissent loses its political meaning, or moral justification, acquiring 'othering' essence when the president places it in the dismissive context of the **fitna**... Following this hard-line speech, the protesters' demands moved from reforming to overthrowing the regime."

— Professor Akeel Abbas on Assad's first public speech after the outbreak of [Syrian Revolution protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_Syrian_Revolution)

[^cite_note-129]

The U.S. imposed limited sanctions against the Assad government in April 2011, followed by [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama)'s executive order as of 18 May 2011 targeting Bashar Assad specifically and six other senior officials.[^cite_note-130][^cite_note-ussanct-131][^cite_note-132] On 23 May 2011, the EU foreign ministers agreed at a meeting in Brussels to add Assad and nine other officials to a list affected by travel bans and asset freezes.[^cite_note-133] On 24 May 2011, Canada imposed sanctions on Syrian leaders, including Assad.[^cite_note-134]

On 20 June, in response to the demands of protesters and international pressure, Assad promised a national dialogue involving movement toward reform, [new parliamentary elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Syrian_parliamentary_election), and greater [freedoms](37071.md). He also urged [refugees](45547.md) to return home from Turkey, while assuring them [amnesty](18940624.md) and blaming all unrest on a small number of saboteurs.[^cite_note-135]

Hundreds of thousands of anti-Assad protesters parade the Syrian flag and shout the [Arab Spring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Spring) slogan [Ash-shab yurid isqat an-nizam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ash-shab_yurid_isqat_an-nizam) (*the people want to bring down the regime!*) in the Assi square, during the [Siege of Hama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Hama_\(2011\)), 22 July 2011.

In July 2011, U.S. Secretary of State [Hillary Clinton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hillary_Clinton) said Assad had "lost legitimacy" as president.[^cite_note-ussanct-131] On 18 August 2011, Barack Obama issued a written statement that urged Assad to "step aside".[^cite_note-mustgo-136][^cite_note-137][^cite_note-whstatem-138] In August, the cartoonist [Ali Farzat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali_Farzat), a critic of Assad's government, was attacked. Relatives of the humourist told media outlets that the attackers threatened to break Farzat's bones as a warning for him to stop drawing cartoons of government officials, particularly Assad. Farzat was hospitalised with fractures in both hands and blunt force trauma to the head.[^cite_note-139][^cite_note-140]

Since October 2011, Russia, as a [permanent member](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Permanent_members_of_the_United_Nations_Security_Council) of the [UN Security Council](31956.md), repeatedly [vetoed](1900272.md) Western-sponsored draft resolutions in the UN Security Council that would have left open the possibility of UN sanctions, or even military intervention, against the Assad government.[^cite_note-guardunlegit-141][^cite_note-Itar-Tass27-1-12-142][^cite_note-143]

On 10 January 2012, Assad gave a speech in which he maintained the uprising was engineered by foreign countries and proclaimed that "victory \[was] near". He also said that the [Arab League](52625.md), by suspending Syria, revealed that it was no longer Arab. However, Assad also said the country would not "close doors" to an Arab-brokered solution if "national sovereignty" was respected. He also said a referendum on a new constitution could be held in March.[^cite_note-144]

By the end of January 2012, it was reported by [Reuters](18998750.md) that over 5,000 civilians and protesters (including armed militants) had been killed by the Syrian army, security agents and militia ([Shabiha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shabiha)), while 1,100 people had been killed by "terrorist armed forces".[^cite_note-145]



Destroyed vehicle on a devastated [Aleppo](159244.md) street, 6 October 2012

On 27 February 2012, Syria claimed that a proposal that a new constitution be drafted received 90% support during the [relevant referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Syrian_constitutional_referendum). The referendum introduced a fourteen-year cumulative term limit for the president of Syria. The referendum was pronounced meaningless by foreign nations including the U.S. and Turkey; the EU announced fresh sanctions against key regime figures.[^cite_note-146] In July 2012, Russian Foreign Minister [Sergey Lavrov](541346.md) denounced Western powers for what he said amounted to blackmail thus provoking a civil war in Syria.[^cite_note-147] On 15 July 2012, the [International Committee of the Red Cross](5023832.md) declared Syria to be in a state of civil war,[^cite_note-148] as the nationwide death toll for all sides was reported to have neared 20,000.[^cite_note-149]

On 6 January 2013, Assad, in his first major speech since June, said that the conflict in his country was due to "enemies" outside of Syria who would "go to Hell" and that they would "be taught a lesson". However, he said that he was still open to a political solution saying that failed attempts at a solution "does not mean we are not interested in a political solution."[^cite_note-150][^cite_note-151] In July 2014, Assad renewed his third term of presidency after voting process conducted in pro-regime territories which were boycotted by the opposition and condemned by the [United Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UN).[^cite_note-theguardian.com-85][^cite_note-telegraph.co.uk-86][^cite_note-Kossaify-87] According to [Joshua Landis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joshua_Landis): "He's (Assad) going to say: 'I am the state, I am Syria, and if the West wants access to Syrians, they have to come through me.'"[^cite_note-telegraph.co.uk-86]

In 2013, reports emerged about an assassination attempt on Bashar that may have motivated a chemical weapons strike by the Assad regime. NBC News reported that the United States conducted investigations to determine whether a failed attempt on his life had influenced the decision to deploy such weapons.[^cite_note-152] During the early stages of the Syrian Civil War in 2012, multiple reports of assassination attempts were made. These allegations coincided with intensifying conflict and growing opposition to Assad's rule.[^cite_note-153]

After the fall of four military bases in September 2014,[^cite_note-AssadWeak-154] which were the last government footholds in the [Raqqa Governorate](5317143.md), Assad received significant criticism from his Alawite base of support.[^cite_note-Dissent2014-155] This included remarks made by Douraid al-Assad, cousin of Bashar al-Assad, demanding the resignation of the Syrian Defence Minister, [Fahd Jassem al-Freij](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fahd_Jassem_al-Freij), following the massacre by the [Islamic State](9087364.md) of hundreds of government troops captured after the IS victory at [Tabqa Airbase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_for_Tabqa_Air_base).[^cite_note-156] This was shortly followed by Alawite protests in [Homs](77533.md) demanding the resignation of the governor,[^cite_note-157] and the dismissal of Assad's cousin [Hafez Makhlouf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hafez_Makhlouf) from his security position leading to his subsequent exile to Belarus.[^cite_note-158] Growing resentment towards Assad among Alawites was fuelled by the disproportionate number of soldiers killed in fighting hailing from Alawite areas,[^cite_note-BurySons-159] a sense that the Assad regime has abandoned them,[^cite_note-160] as well as the failing economic situation.[^cite_note-AlawitesVoice-161] Figures close to Assad began voicing concerns regarding the likelihood of its survival, with one saying in late 2014; "I don't see the current situation as sustainable ... I think Damascus will collapse at some point."[^cite_note-AssadWeak-154]



Military situation in September 2015

In 2015, several members of the Assad family died in [Latakia](195594.md) under unclear circumstances.[^cite_note-162] On 14 March, an influential cousin of Assad and founder of the [shabiha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shabiha), Mohammed Toufic al-Assad, was assassinated with five bullets to the head in a dispute over influence in [Qardaha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qardaha) – the [ancestral home](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancestral_home) of the Assad family.[^cite_note-163] In April 2015, Assad ordered the arrest of his cousin Munther al-Assad in Alzirah, Latakia.[^cite_note-164] It remains unclear whether the arrest was due to actual crimes.[^cite_note-165]

After a string of government defeats in northern and southern Syria, analysts noted growing government instability coupled with continued waning support for the Assad government among its core Alawite base of support.[^cite_note-166] Reports indicated that Assad's relatives, Alawites, and businessmen were increasingly fleeing Damascus for Latakia and foreign countries.[^cite_note-167][^cite_note-168] Intelligence chief [Ali Mamlouk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali_Mamlouk) was placed under house arrest sometime in April and stood accused of plotting with Assad's exiled uncle [Rifaat al-Assad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rifaat_al-Assad) to replace Bashar as president.[^cite_note-169] Further high-profile deaths included the commanders of the Fourth Armoured Division, the Belli military airbase, the army's special forces and of the First Armoured Division, with an errant air strike during the [Palmyra offensive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palmyra_offensive_\(May_2015\)) killing two officers who were reportedly related to Assad.[^cite_note-170]

#### Presidency after Russian intervention (2015–2024)

See also: [Russian involvement in the Syrian civil war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_involvement_in_the_Syrian_civil_war) and [Foreign involvement in the Syrian civil war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_involvement_in_the_Syrian_civil_war)

On 4 September 2015, when prospects of Assad's survival looked bleak, Russian President [Vladimir Putin](32817.md) said that Russia was providing the Assad government with sufficiently "serious" help: with both logistical and military support.[^cite_note-171][^cite_note-172][^cite_note-Borshchevskaya_2022_69–88-173] Shortly after the start of [direct military intervention by Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_military_intervention_in_the_Syrian_Civil_War) on 30 September 2015 at the formal request of the Syrian government, Putin stated the military operation had been thoroughly prepared in advance and defined Russia's goal in Syria as "stabilising the legitimate power in Syria and creating the conditions for political compromise".[^cite_note-goalstabilis-174] Putin's intervention saved the Assad regime at a time when it was on the verge of a looming collapse. It also enabled Moscow to achieve its key geo-strategic objectives such as total control of Syrian airspace, naval bases that granted permanent martial reach across the [Eastern Mediterranean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Mediterranean) and easier access to intervene in Libya.[^cite_note-Borshchevskaya_2022_69–88-173]

In November 2015, Assad reiterated that a diplomatic process to bring the country's civil war to an end could not begin while it was occupied by "terrorists", although it was considered by [BBC News](1139893.md) to be unclear whether he meant only [ISIL](9087364.md) or Western-supported rebels as well.[^cite_note-175] On 22 November, Assad said that within two months of its air campaign Russia had achieved more in its fight against ISIL than the [U.S.-led coalition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American-led_intervention_in_Syria) had achieved in a year.[^cite_note-176] In an interview with [Czech Television](1910176.md) on 1 December, he said that the leaders who demanded his resignation were of no interest to him, as nobody takes them seriously because they are "shallow" and controlled by the United States.[^cite_note-177][^cite_note-178] At the end of December 2015, senior U.S. officials privately admitted that Russia had achieved its central goal of stabilising Syria and, with the expenses relatively low, could sustain the operation at this level for years to come.[^cite_note-reutersstabilis-179]

In December 2015, Putin stated that Russia was supporting Assad's forces and was ready to back anti-Assad rebels in a joint fight against IS.[^cite_note-180] On 22 January 2016, the *[Financial Times](136566.md)*, citing anonymous "senior western intelligence officials", claimed that Russian general [Igor Sergun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Igor_Sergun), the director of [GRU](261749.md), the Main Intelligence Directorate of the General Staff of the [Armed Forces of the Russian Federation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armed_Forces_of_the_Russian_Federation), had shortly before his sudden death on 3 January 2016 been sent to Damascus with a message from Vladimir Putin asking that President Assad step aside.[^cite_note-ftresign-181] The *Financial Times'* report was denied by Putin's spokesman.[^cite_note-182]

It was reported in December 2016 that Assad's forces had retaken half of rebel-held [Aleppo](159244.md), ending a 6-year stalemate in the city.[^cite_note-183][^cite_note-184] On 15 December, as it was reported government forces were on the brink of retaking all of Aleppo – a "turning point" in the civil war, Assad celebrated the "liberation" of the city, and stated, "History is being written by every Syrian citizen."[^cite_note-185]

After the election of [Donald Trump](4848272.md), the priority of the U.S. concerning Assad was unlike the priority of the [Obama administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Barack_Obama#Syrian_civil_war), and in March 2017, [U.S. Ambassador to the UN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Ambassador_to_the_United_Nations) [Nikki Haley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikki_Haley) stated the U.S. was no longer focused on "getting Assad out",[^cite_note-186] but this position changed in the wake of the [2017 Khan Shaykhun chemical attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Khan_Shaykhun_chemical_attack).[^cite_note-187] Following the [missile strikes on a Syrian airbase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Shayrat_missile_strike) on the orders of President Trump, Assad's spokesperson described the U.S.' behaviour as "unjust and arrogant aggression" and stated that the missile strikes "do not change the deep policies" of the Syrian government.[^cite_note-188] President Assad also told the [Agence France-Presse](471981.md) that Syria's military had given up all its chemical weapons in 2013, and would not have used them if they still retained any, and stated that the chemical attack was a "100 percent fabrication" used to justify a U.S. airstrike.[^cite_note-189] In June 2017, Russian President Putin said "Assad didn't use the \[chemical weapons]" and that the chemical attack was "done by people who wanted to blame him for that".[^cite_note-190] UN and international chemical weapons inspectors from the [Organisation for the Prohibition of Chemical Weapons](36832.md) (OPCW) found the attack was the work of the Assad regime.[^cite_note-CNN_sarin_report-191]

On 7 November 2017, the Syrian government announced that it had signed the [Paris Climate Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_Climate_Agreement).[^cite_note-192] In May 2018, it recognised the independence of Russian-occupied separatist republics of [Abkhazia](18933375.md) and [South Ossetia](318381.md) in Georgia, leading to backlash from the [European Union](9317.md), [United States](3434750.md), [Canada](5042916.md) and other countries.[^cite_note-Deutsche_Welle-193][^cite_note-194][^cite_note-195] On 30 August 2020, the [First Hussein Arnous government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Hussein_Arnous_government) was formed, which included a new [Council of Ministers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Ministers_\(Syria\)).[^cite_note-196] Lebanese general [Abbas Ibrahim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abbas_Ibrahim_\(general\)) alleges that around 2020, Assad refused an offer by the United States to lift [sanctions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caesar_Syria_Civilian_Protection_Act) and remove [troops](43683196.md) in Syria in exchange for [Austin Tice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austin_Tice)'s whereabouts.[^cite_note-theatlantic-197]

##### Re-election and support for Russian invasion of Ukraine

In the [2021 presidential elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Syrian_presidential_election) held on 26 May, Assad secured his fourth 7-year tenure; by winning 95.2% of the eligible votes. The elections were boycotted by the [opposition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_opposition_to_Bashar_al-Assad) and [SDF](48205248.md); while the refugees and internally displaced citizens were disqualified to vote; enabling only 38% of Syrians to participate in the process. Independent international observers as well as representatives of Western countries described the elections as a farce. [United Nations](31769.md) condemned the elections for directly violating Resolution 2254; and announced that it has "no mandate".[^cite_note-198][^cite_note-199][^cite_note-200][^cite_note-201][^cite_note-auto-202]



Assad with Iranian President [Ebrahim Raisi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ebrahim_Raisi) in Damascus, 3 May 2023

On 10 August 2021, the [Second Hussein Arnous government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Hussein_Arnous_government) was formed.[^cite_note-203] Under Assad, Syria became a strong supporter of the [2022 Russian invasion of Ukraine](79400268.md) and was one of the five countries that opposed the [UN General Assembly](31957.md) resolution denouncing the invasion, which called upon Russia to pull back its troops. Three days prior to the invasion, Foreign Minister [Faisal Mekdad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Faisal_Mekdad) was dispatched to Moscow to affirm Syria's recognition of [Donetsk](42425747.md) and [Luhansk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luhansk_People's_Republic) separatist republics. A day after the invasion, Assad praised the invasion as "a correction of history and a restoration of balance in the [global order](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_order) after the [fall of the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dissolution_of_the_Soviet_Union)" in a phone call with [Vladimir Putin](32817.md).[^cite_note-204][^cite_note-205][^cite_note-206] Syria became the first country after Russia to officially recognise the "independence and sovereignty" of the two breakaway regions in June 2022.[^cite_note-207][^cite_note-208][^cite_note-209] Syria formally broke its diplomatic ties to Ukraine on 20 July 2022, citing the [principle of reciprocity](1046231.md).[^cite_note-210]

On the 12th anniversary of beginning of the protests of [Syrian Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_Syrian_Revolution), Assad held a meeting with [Vladimir Putin](32817.md) during an official visit to Russia. In a televised broadcast with Putin, Assad defended Russia's "special military operation" as a war against "[neo-Nazis](54361.md) and [old Nazis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banderite)" of Ukraine.[^cite_note-211][^cite_note-rferl.org-212] He recognised the [Russian annexation of four Ukrainian oblasts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_annexation_of_Donetsk,_Kherson,_Luhansk_and_Zaporizhzhia_oblasts) and ratified the new Russian borders, claiming that the territories were "historically Russian". Assad also urged Russia to expand its [military presence in Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_military_intervention_in_the_Syrian_civil_war) by establishing new bases and deploying more troops on the ground, strengthening its military role in Syria.[^cite_note-218]



Military situation before the [opposition offensives](78460726.md) in late 2024.\
Territories held by the [SDF](48205248.md) (yellow), [IS](9087364.md) (grey), the [Syrian Army](3423927.md) (red), the [SNA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_National_Army) and [Turkey](11125639.md) (light green), [Tahrir al-Sham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tahrir_al-Sham) (white), the [SFA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolutionary_Commando_Army) and the [United States](3434750.md) (teal).

##### Regional normalization initiatives

See also: [Arab normalization with Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_normalization_with_Syria)

In March 2023, Assad visited the [United Arab Emirates](69328.md) and met with UAE's President [Mohamed bin Zayed Al Nahyan](2121945.md).[^cite_note-219] In May 2023, he attended the [Arab League summit in Jeddah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Arab_League_summit), [Saudi Arabia](349303.md), where he was welcomed by Saudi Crown Prince [Mohammed bin Salman](38689358.md).[^cite_note-220] He met with Egyptian President [Abdel Fattah el-Sisi](36703624.md) and other Arab leaders.[^cite_note-221] Following a year of [normalization attempts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian%E2%80%93Turkish_normalization), Turkish President Erdoğan expressed in July 2023 readiness to meet Assad, but rejected withdrawal from northern Syria as a condition.[^cite_note-222] Assad insisted that any meeting could not occur on Turkey's terms and maintained his demand for full Turkish withdrawal.[^cite_note-223]

In September 2023, Assad attended the [Asian Games](39205.md) opening ceremony in [Hangzhou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hangzhou) and met with Chinese President [Xi Jinping](2017814.md).[^cite_note-224] They announced the establishment of a China–Syria strategic partnership.[^cite_note-225] In November 2023, Assad attended the [Arab–Islamic extraordinary summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Arab%E2%80%93Islamic_extraordinary_summit) in Riyadh.[^cite_note-226] In May 2024, he attended the Arab League summit in [Bahrain](18933277.md).[^cite_note-227] In this time period the government refused any major incentives for reconciliation and repatriation of the "former" [rebel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_rebel) groups. Bashar was encouraged to do so by its allies, such as those in the [Axis of Resistance](36658251.md), but he did not follow up on these suggestions leaving the society polarised.[^cite_note-\:7-88]

## Overthrow and exile

### Regime collapse

See also: [2024 Syrian opposition offensives](78460726.md) and [Fall of the Assad regime](78548066.md)



[Syrian opposition offensives](78460726.md) that overthrew Assad's regime in 11 days

In December 2024, the Islamist group [Hayat Tahrir al-Sham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tahrir_al-Sham) (HTS), [took control of Aleppo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Aleppo_\(2024\)), prompting a retaliatory airstrike campaign by President Assad and his Russian allies. The strikes, which targeted population centers and several hospitals in the [rebel-held](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Insurgency_in_Idlib) city of [Idlib](2528679.md), resulted in at least 25 deaths, according to the [White Helmets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Helmets_\(Syria\)) rescue group. [NATO](21133.md) countries issued a joint statement calling for the protection of civilians and critical infrastructure to prevent further displacement and ensure humanitarian access. They stressed the urgent need for a Syrian-led political solution, in accordance with [United Nations Security Council Resolution 2254](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Security_Council_Resolution_2254), which advocates for dialogue between the Syrian government and opposition forces. The [rebel offensive](78460726.md), which had begun on 27 November 2024, continued its [advance into Hama Governorate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Hama_offensive) following their capture of Aleppo.[^cite_note-228][^cite_note-229][^cite_note-230]

On 4 December 2024, fierce clashes erupted in [Hama Governorate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hama_Governorate) as the Syrian army engaged Islamist-led insurgents in a bid to halt their advance on the key city of [Hama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hama). Government forces claimed to have launched a counteroffensive with air support, pushing back rebel factions, including Hayat Tahrir al-Sham (HTS), around 6 miles (9.7 km) from the city. However, despite reinforcements, the rebels captured the city on 5 December.[^cite_note-231] The fighting led to widespread displacement, with nearly 50,000 people fleeing the area and over 600 casualties reported, including 104 civilians.[^cite_note-232] On 6 December, rebels began encircling the capital Damascus.[^cite_note-233] On 7 December, rebels captured the strategic city of Homs, cutting off Damascus from regime strongholds on the coast.[^cite_note-234] A few hours later in the early morning of 8 December, Assad fled Damascus just before [its fall to the rebels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_Damascus_\(2024\)),[^cite_note-235] ending 54 years of the Assad family's rule.[^cite_note-236] In the final hours of his rule, he reportedly abandoned the capital after Russian officials showed him footage indicating regime forces were collapsing, taking only a few aides and his son Hafez while leaving others behind. As he departed, his longtime driver expressed disappointment, asking, "You're really leaving us?" to which Assad replied, "What about you people? Aren't you going to fight?", an episode observers cited as emblematic of his detachment and unwillingness to assume responsibility for the collapse of his authority.[^cite_note-theatlantic-197] The Russian government said that Assad had "stepped down" as president following a personal decision and had left Syria.[^cite_note-237][^cite_note-238] Following efforts by Russian foreign minister [Sergey Lavrov](541346.md) to facilitate his departure, Assad, who left under great secrecy, was reported to have gone first to the Russian-operated [Khmeimim Air Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khmeimim_Air_Base) near [Latakia](195594.md) before proceeding to Moscow.[^cite_note-239] [Interfax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interfax), quoting an unnamed source at the [Kremlin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kremlin), said that Assad and his family had arrived in Moscow and that the Russian government had granted them [asylum](1621432.md) "on humanitarian grounds".[^cite_note-\:2-22]

On 16 December, the [Telegram](41501305.md) account of the Syrian presidency published a statement attributed to Assad saying that he had gone to a Russian military base in [Latakia Governorate](7445289.md) "to oversee combat operations" following the fall of Damascus but was evacuated out of the country by Russia after coming under siege from rebel forces, adding that he had no intention of resigning or going into exile.[^cite_note-240]

### Exile in Moscow

On 7 April 2025, Russian Ambassador to Iraq [Elbrus Kutrashev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elbrus_Kutrashev) told the *[Islamic Republic News Agency](841314.md)* that Assad's settlement in Moscow was conditional on his total withdrawal from media and political activities. He added that the asylum granted to Assad and his family had been personally ordered by Russian president [Vladimir Putin](32817.md) and would remain unchanged.[^cite_note-241] In an interview with *[The New York Times](30680.md)* in April 2025, Syrian president [Ahmed al-Sharaa](44007699.md) said that Syrian officials requested Russia to [extradite Assad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proposed_handover_of_Bashar_al-Assad_to_Syria) as a condition for allowing their military presence in Syria, but Russia refused.[^cite_note-242]

On 20 September 2025, while in exile in Moscow, Assad was hospitalized in critical condition. The [Syrian Observatory for Human Rights](34029669.md) has alleged he was poisoned in his home, though not specifying whether this was intentional or accidental. He was eventually discharged nine days later.[^cite_note-243] On 13 October 2025, Lavrov denied reports that Assad was poisoned, saying Russia granted asylum to Assad and his family for humanitarian reasons and that Assad "has no issues living in our capital."[^cite_note-244]

On 8 October 2025, the German outlet *[Die Zeit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Die_Zeit)* reported that Assad and his family had settled into a luxury [skyscraper](29485.md) apartment, where he reportedly made occasional visits to a shopping mall and spent much of his time playing [online games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Online_game) while remaining under Putin's protection and control.[^cite_note-245][^cite_note-246][^cite_note-247] *[The Guardian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Guardian)* reported that Assad was likely residing in the upscale neighbourhood of [Rublyovka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rublyovka), had returned to his [medical training](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medical_education), and was back in the classroom, studying [Russian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_language) and taking [ophthalmology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ophthalmology) lessons.[^cite_note-248]

During a meeting with Putin on 15 October 2025, Syrian President al-Sharaa requested the extradition of Assad to Syria.[^cite_note-249]

In June 2026, the *[Yedioth Ahronoth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yedioth_Ahronoth)* newspaper stated that the family had requested relocation to [Abu Dhabi](18950756.md); however, the UAE refused due to security considerations, such as the threat of assassination or being targeted.[^cite_note-250]

## Controversies

### Corruption

Main article: [Corruption in Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corruption_in_Syria)

See also: [Economy of Syria](27476.md)

At the onset of the [Syrian revolution](39750126.md), corruption in Syria was endemic, and the country was ranked 129th in the 2011 [Corruption Perceptions Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corruption_Perceptions_Index).[^cite_note-251] Since the 1970s, Syria's economy has been dominated by the patronage networks of Ba'ath party elites and [Alawite](436616.md) loyalists of the Assad family, who established control over Syria's public sectors based on kinship and nepotism. The pervasive nature of corruption had been a source of controversy within the Ba'ath party circles and the wider public; as early as the 1980s.[^cite_note-252] Bashar al-Assad's economic liberalisation program during the 2000s became a symbol of corruption and nepotism, as the scheme's beneficiaries were Alawite loyalists who seized a significant portion of the privatised sectors and business assets. The government's actions alienated the vast majority of the Syrian public, particularly the rural and urban working classes, who strongly disliked the resulting economic disparities that became overtly visible.[^cite_note-Allam,_Saber_2019_26–27-40][^cite_note-France_24-92] Assad's cousin [Rami Makhlouf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rami_Makhlouf) was the regime's most favored oligarch during this period, marked by the institutionalisation of corruption, handicapping of small businesses and casting down private [entrepreneurship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Entrepreneurship).[^cite_note-253] The persistence of corruption, sectarian bias towards Alawites, nepotism and widespread bribery that existed in party, bureaucracy and military led to popular anger that resulted in the eruption of the 2011 Syrian Revolution. The protests were the most fierce in working-class neighbourhoods, which had long bore the brunt of the regime's exploitation policies that privileged its own loyalists.[^cite_note-254][^cite_note-255]

According to [ABC News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ABC_News_\(United_States\)), as a result of the Syrian civil war, "government-controlled Syria is truncated in size, battered and impoverished."[^cite_note-256] Economic sanctions (the [Syria Accountability Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syria_Accountability_Act)) were applied long before the Syrian civil war by the U.S. and were joined by the EU at the outbreak of the civil war, causing disintegration of the Syrian economy.[^cite_note-257] These sanctions were reinforced in October 2014 by the EU and U.S.[^cite_note-258][^cite_note-259] Industry in parts of the country that are still held by the government is heavily state-controlled, with [economic liberalisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_liberalisation) being reversed during the current conflict.[^cite_note-260] The [London School of Economics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/London_School_of_Economics) has stated that as a result of the Syrian civil war, a [war economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_economy) has developed in Syria.[^cite_note-261] A 2014 [European Council on Foreign Relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Council_on_Foreign_Relations) report also stated that a war economy has formed:

> Three years into a conflict that is estimated to have killed at least 140,000 people from both sides, much of the Syrian economy lies in ruins. As the violence has expanded and sanctions have been imposed, assets and infrastructure have been destroyed, economic output has fallen, and investors have fled the country. Unemployment now exceeds 50 percent and half of the population lives below the poverty line ... against this backdrop, a war economy is emerging that is creating significant new economic networks and business activities that feed off the violence, chaos, and lawlessness gripping the country. This war economy – to which Western sanctions have inadvertently contributed – is creating incentives for some Syrians to prolong the conflict and making it harder to end it.[^cite_note-262]

A [UN](31769.md) commissioned report by the Syrian Centre for Policy Research states that two-thirds of the Syrian population now lives in "extreme poverty".[^cite_note-263] Unemployment stands at 50 percent.[^cite_note-SurvivalMode-264] In October 2014, a \$50 million mall opened in [Tartus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tartus) which provoked criticism from government supporters and was seen as part of an Assad government policy of attempting to project a sense of normalcy throughout the civil war.[^cite_note-265] A government policy to give preference to families of slain soldiers for government jobs was cancelled after it caused an uproar[^cite_note-BurySons-159] while rising accusations of corruption caused protests.[^cite_note-AlawitesVoice-161] In December 2014, the EU banned sales of jet fuel to the Assad government, forcing the government to buy more expensive uninsured jet fuel shipments in the future.[^cite_note-266]

Taking advantage of the increased role of the state as a result of the civil war, Bashar and his wife Asma have begun annexing Syria's economic assets from their loyalists, seeking to displace the old business elites and monopolise their direct control of the economy. [Maher al-Assad](3139640.md), the brother of Bashar, has also become wealthy by overseeing the operations of Syria's state-sponsored [captagon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Captagon) drug industry and seizing much of the spoils of war. The ruling couple currently owns vast swathes of Syria's shipping, real estate, telecommunications and banking sectors.[^cite_note-267][^cite_note-268] Significant changes have been happening to Syrian economy since the government's confiscation campaigns launched in 2019, which involved major economic assets being transferred to the Presidential couple to project their power and influence. Particularly noteworthy dynamic has been the rise of [Asma al-Assad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asma_al-Assad), who heads Syria's clandestine economic council and is thought to have become "a central funnel of economic power in Syria". Through her Syria Trust NGO, the backbone of her financial network, Asma vets the foreign aid coming to Syria; since the government authorises UN organisations only if it works under state agencies.[^cite_note-269]

Corruption has been rising sporadically in recent years, with Syria being considered the most corrupt country in the [Arab World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_World).[^cite_note-270][^cite_note-271] As of 2022, Syria is the ranked second worst globally in the [Corruption Perceptions Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corruption_Perceptions_Index).[^cite_note-272]

### Sectarianism

See also: [Sectarianism and minorities in the Syrian civil war](34766543.md)

Hafez al-Assad's government was widely counted amongst the most repressive Arab [dictatorships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dictatorship) of the 20th century. As Bashar inherited his father's mantle, he sought to implement "authoritarian upgrading" by purging those from his father's generation and staffing the party and military with loyalist Alawite officers, further entrenching the sectarianism within the system.[^cite_note-273][^cite_note-auto2-274] While officially the Ba'athist government adheres to a strict secularist doctrine, in practice it has implemented sectarian engineering policies in the society to suppress dissent and monopolise its absolute power.[^cite_note-275]

> "During Hafez-al-Assad's reign, he resorted to emphasising the sectarian identities that the previous [Ba'ath Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ba'ath_Party) rejected; believing the only way to ensure stability was through building a trusted security force... Hafez pursued a strategy to "make the Alawite community a loyal monolith while keeping Syria's Sunni majority divided". Yet Syria became a [police state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Police_state), enforcing stability through threat of brute force repression... Bashar had already followed in his father's footsteps, carefully manoeuvring his most loyal allies into the military-security apparatus, government ministries and the [Ba'ath party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Socialist_Ba'ath_Party_%E2%80%93_Syria_Region)."

— Antonia Robson

[^cite_note-Robson-276]

The regime has attempted to portray itself to the outside world as "the protector of minorities" and instills the fear of the majority rule in the society to mobilise loyalists from minorities.[^cite_note-NPR_APRIL_2015-277] Assad loyalist figures like [Michel Samaha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michel_Samaha) have advocated sectarian mobilisation to defend the regime from what he labelled as the "sea of [Sunnis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunnis)". Assad regime has unleashed sectarian violence through private Alawite militias like the *[Shabiha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shabiha)*, particularly in Sunni areas. Alawite religious iconography and communal sentiments are common themes used by [Alawite](436616.md) warrior-shaykhs who lead the Alawite militias; as justification to commit massacres, abductions and torture in opposition strongholds.[^cite_note-278] Various development policies adopted by the regime had followed a sectarian pattern. An urbanisation scheme implemented by the government in the city of Homs led to expulsions of thousands of Sunni residents during the 2000s, while Alawite majority areas were left intact.[^cite_note-279]

Even as [Syrian Ba'athism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_Ba'athism) absorbed diverse communal identities into the homogenous unifying discourse of the state; socio-political power became monopolised by Alawite loyalists. Despite officially adhering to non-confessionalism, [Syrian Armed Forces](27479.md) have also been institutionally sectarianised. While the conscripts and lower-ranks are overwhelmingly non-Alawite, the higher ranks are packed by Alawite loyalists who effectively control the logistics and security policy. Elite units of the Syrian military such as the [Tiger Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tiger_Forces), [Republican Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_Guard_\(Syria\)), [4th Armoured Division](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/4th_Armoured_Division_\(Syria\)), etc. regarded by the government as crucial for its survival; are composed mostly of Alawites. Sunni officers are under constant surveillance by the secret police, with most of them being assigned with Alawite assistants who monitor their movements. Pro-regime [paramilitary](146730.md) groups such as the [National Defense Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Defense_Force_\(Syria\)) are also organised around sectarian loyalty to the Ba'athist government. During the [Syrian Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_Syrian_Revolution) uprisings, the [Ba'athist government](57134800.md) deployed a securitisation strategy that depended on sectarian mobilisation, unleashing violence on protestors and extensive crackdowns across the country, prompting opposition groups to turn to armed revolt. Syrian society was further sectarianised following the [Iranian intervention in the Syrian civil war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_involvement_in_the_Syrian_civil_war), which witnessed numerous [Khomeinist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khomeinism) militant groups sponsored by Iran fight in the side of the Assad government.[^cite_note-280][^cite_note-Robson-276]

### Human rights

See also: [Human rights in Ba'athist Syria](78548501.md)

&#x20;The Ba'athist government ruled Syria as a totalitarian state, policing every aspect of Syrian society until 2024.[^cite_note-281] The president made key decisions with counsel from a small number of security advisors, ministers, and senior members of the ruling Ba'ath Party, with scant regard for punishing, arresting, or prosecuting officials who violated human rights.[^cite_note-282] The surveillance system of the *[Mukhabarat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mukhabarat)* was pervasive, with the total number of agents working for its various branches estimated to be as high as 1\:158 ratio with the civilian population. Security services shut down civil society organisations, curtailed freedom of movement within the country and banned non-Ba'athist political literature and symbols.[^cite_note-ifimes.org-120][^cite_note-Hill-283] In 2010, [Human Rights Watch](46530.md) published the report "*A Wasted Decade*" documenting repression during Assad's first decade of [emergency rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emergency_rule); marked by arbitrary arrests, censorship and discrimination against [Syrian Kurds](3821855.md).[^cite_note-Hill-283][^cite_note-hrw\.org-284]



Billboard with a portrait of Bashar al-Assad and the text 'Syria is protected by God' on the [old city wall of Damascus](8914.md#Walls_and_gates_of_Damascus) in 2006

Throughout the 2000s, *[Mukhabarat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mukhabarat)* agents carried out routine [abductions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forced_abduction), [arbitrary detentions](13435705.md) and [torture](47702.md) of civilians. Numerous [show trials](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Show_trial) were conducted against dissidents, filling Syrian prisons with journalists and human rights activists. Members of Syria's [General Intelligence Directorate](19204147.md) had long enjoyed broad privileges to carry out extrajudicial actions and they have immunity from criminal offences. In 2008, Assad extended this immunity to other departments of security forces.[^cite_note-hrw\.org-284] Human Rights groups, such as [Human Rights Watch](46530.md) and [Amnesty International](18947898.md), have detailed how the Assad government's [secret police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secret_police) tortured, imprisoned, and killed political opponents, and those who speak out against the government.[^cite_note-285][^cite_note-286] In addition, some 600 Lebanese political prisoners were thought to be held in government prisons since the [Syrian occupation of Lebanon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_occupation_of_Lebanon), with some held for as long as over 30 years.[^cite_note-287] From 2006, the Assad government expanded the use of travel bans against political dissidents.[^cite_note-288] In an interview with [ABC News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ABC_News_\(United_States\)) in 2007, Assad stated: "We don't have such \[things as] political prisoners," though *[The New York Times](30680.md)* reported the arrest of 30 Syrian political dissidents who were organising a joint opposition front in December 2007, with 3 members of this group considered to be opposition leaders being remanded in custody.[^cite_note-289]

The government also denied permission for human rights organisations and independent NGOs to work in the country.[^cite_note-hrw\.org-284] In 2010, Syria banned [face veils](68897.md) at universities.[^cite_note-290][^cite_note-291] Following the protests of the [Syrian revolution](39750126.md) in 2011, Assad partially relaxed the veil ban.[^cite_note-292]



Demonstration in [Montreal](7954681.md) in solidarity with the people of Syria. The sign reads: "Stop torture and inhumane treatment of prisoners in Syria!"

*[Foreign Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_Affairs)* journal released an editorial on the Syrian situation in the wake of the 2011 protests:[^cite_note-The_Sturdy_House_That_Assad_Built-293]

> During its decades of rule... the Assad family developed a strong political safety net by firmly integrating the military into the government. In 1970, Hafez al-Assad, Bashar's father, seized power after rising through the ranks of the [Syrian armed forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_armed_forces), during which time he established a network of loyal [Alawites](436616.md) by installing them in key posts. In fact, the military, ruling elite, and [ruthless secret police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_Intelligence_Directorate_\(Syria\)) are so intertwined that it is now impossible to separate the [Assad regime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assad_regime) from the security establishment. Bashar al-Assad's threat to use force against protesters would be more plausible than Tunisia's or Egypt's were. So, unlike in Tunisia and Egypt, where a professionally trained military tended to play an independent role, the regime and its loyal forces have been able to deter all but the most resolute and fearless oppositional activists... At the same time, it is significantly different from [Libya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Socialist_People's_Libyan_Arab_Jamahiriya_\(1977%E2%80%932011\)), where the military, although brutal and loyal to the regime, is a more disorganized group of militant thugs than a trained and disciplined army.

Under Ba'athist rule, non-Arab ethnic groups in Syrian society were heavily marginalized. Ethnic minority groups in Syria like the [Kurds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_Kurds), [Turkmen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_Turkmen), [Circassians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_Circassians), [Chechens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chechens_in_Syria), etc. were systematically persecuted and oppressed under discriminatory state policies. The [2012 constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Constitution_of_Ba'athist_Syria) pushed by the Assad regime, which was widely criticized by Syrian opposition and civil society activists, entrenched the discriminatory [Arab nationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_nationalism) policies of the Ba'athist system. Languages other than [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic) were not recognized in the Ba'athist constitutions; and Ba'ath party's front groups such as the "Ba'ath Vanguard" and "[Shabibat al-Thawra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolutionary_Youth_Union)" indoctrinated students with racist and chauvinist ideas in Syrian state educational institutions.[^cite_note-294]

Kurds, in particular, were heavily repressed and systemically targeted by the state apparatus. Hundreds of thousands of [Syrian Kurds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_Kurds) were stripped of citizenship, and several Kurdish localities were Arabized. Assad regime also imposed several restrictions upon Syrian Kurds from speaking the Kurdish language, and launched crackdowns against those who taught the [Kurdish language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurdish_language) privately.
